I… I came in because I just can’t shake this heavy feeling. Like this hopeless weight, you know? Ever since the divorce I kept trying to rebuild things—work, the apartment, routines, all of it—and now it feels like everything I built has just collapsed, and I’m sitting underneath it.

Ever since the divorce I kept trying to rebuild things—work, the apartment, routines, all of it—and now it feels like everything I built has just collapsed, and I’m sitting underneath it.\n\nSorry, that sounds dramatic. I’m an engineer, so I keep thinking of it like a structure failing, which is probably not helpful, but… that’s how it feels. Like I’m trapped under the debris and I don’t know how to get out.\n\nDoctor: Did this heavy, hopeless feeling start after the divorce?\n\nPatient: Sort of… not right after the divorce itself, exactly. It’s been more like the past month that it’s really been hanging over me.\n\nThe divorce set things off, I guess, but the financial mess afterward—sorting accounts, bills, realizing what I could and couldn’t afford—that’s when it really sank in. I remember looking at this spreadsheet I made, color-coded and everything, like somehow formatting it nicely would make it less awful, and it just… didn’t. But the feeling is this heavy, hopeless thing, like I’m stuck under everything that fell apart and I can’t really get out from under it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / civil_engineer / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. I don’t know… I mean, I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way. I’ve still been doing some things, but it’s hard to tell if I’m enjoying them or just… going through the motions.

Like, I used to spend ages messing with little design projects at home—totally unnecessary stuff, like reorganizing a shelf so it “distributes load better,” which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud. And lately I’ll start something and then get distracted by, I don’t know, a bill sitting on the counter or an email I forgot to answer, and then suddenly I’m cleaning a drawer I didn’t need to clean.

So… I’m not sure. Maybe? I just don’t feel very connected to much right now.

Yeah… it has. More than I wanted to admit at first.

Work used to be the place where I could sort of hold myself together because there were drawings and numbers and decisions that had a process, you know? But lately I’ve been making calls I probably shouldn’t have been making—questionable ones—and then I’d realize afterward I wasn’t thinking clearly. And meetings… I’ve just kind of fallen apart in them. Not in some dramatic movie way, just losing the thread, getting overwhelmed, not being able to answer things I should know.

My supervisor actually took me off active project decisions, which was humiliating. I mean, I get why. You can’t have someone unstable making calls about loads and safety and budgets and all that. Sorry, that’s probably too much detail.

And at home it’s not better. I stopped keeping up with basic routines, like the normal maintenance stuff of being a person. It got bad enough that I ended up being sent to the emergency department because I didn’t feel safe with my thoughts.

honestly believed I could outthink the whole mess, fix my finances, save a couple of troubled projects at work, and maybe launch myself as some kind of sustainability expert all at once.\n- key: manifestation: I started pushing myself into extra proposals, calling colleagues with new ideas, volunteering for tasks no one asked me to take on, and pacing around my apartment when there was external stimuli.\n  manifestation: In meetings my attention kept jumping from the HVAC noise to someone’s pen tapping to a random idea about a site layout, and I’d lose the thread of what we were S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I’ve been thinking that it would be easier if I didn’t manifestation: I started pushing myself into extra proposals, calling colleagues with new ideas, volunteering for tasks no one asked me to take on, and pacing around my apartment when there was I think it’s more than what I’d expect from just the divorce and money stuff.\n\nI mean, those things are terrible, obviously, and I could make a whole spreadsheet of the practical reasons I’m stressed — I’ve always done that, lists and budgets and little color-coded tabs, which probably sounds ridiculous — but this feels different. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I… I came in because I just can’t shake this heavy feeling. Like this hopeless weight, you know? Ever since the divorce I kept trying to rebuild things—work, the apartment, routines, all of it—and now it feels like everything I built has just collapsed, and I’m sitting underneath it.\n\nSorry, that sounds dramatic. I’m an engineer, so I keep thinking of it like a structure failing, which is probably not helpful, but… that’s how it feels. Like I’m trapped under the debris and I don’t know how to get out.\n\nDoctor: Did this heavy, hopeless feeling start after the divorce?\n\nPatient: Sort of… not right after the divorce itself, exactly. It’s been more like the past month that it’s really been hanging over me.\n\nThe divorce set things off, I guess, but the financial mess afterward—sorting accounts, bills, realizing what I could and couldn’t afford—that’s when it really sank in. I remember looking at this spreadsheet I made, color-coded and everything, like somehow formatting it nicely would make it less awful, and it just… didn’t. Since around then, it’s been there almost every day.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem mainly connected to the financial fallout from the divorce?\n\nPatient: I mean… the money part is definitely wrapped up in it, but it’s bigger than just finances. It’s like after the divorce I kept trying to rebuild everything piece by piece, like if I just made a plan and followed it, I’d be okay. And now it feels like all of that collapsed anyway.\n\nSorry, I keep going back to construction metaphors—it’s just how my brain works. But the feeling is this heavy, hopeless thing, like I’m stuck under everything that fell apart and I can’t really get out from under it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way. I’ve still been doing some things, but it’s hard to tell if I’m enjoying them or just… going through the motions.\n\nLike, I used to spend ages messing with little design projects at home—totally unnecessary stuff, like reorganizing a shelf so it “distributes load better,” which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud. And lately I’ll start something and then get distracted by, I don’t know, a bill sitting on the counter or an email I forgot to answer, and then suddenly I’m cleaning a drawer I didn’t need to clean.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Maybe? I just don’t feel very connected to much right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think so. It’s this heavy, hopeless feeling I can’t seem to shake.\n\nI came in because it feels like everything I tried to build back up after the divorce just collapsed, and I’m kind of… sitting underneath it. Like when a structure fails and you can see afterward there were too many loads on it, too many weak points, but knowing that doesn’t actually get you out from under the rubble. So yes, I guess—down, depressed, whatever word fits—it’s there a lot of the day.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. More than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nWork used to be the place where I could sort of hold myself together because there were drawings and numbers and decisions that had a process, you know? But lately I’ve been making calls I probably shouldn’t have been making—questionable ones—and then I’d realize afterward I wasn’t thinking clearly. And meetings… I’ve just kind of fallen apart in them. Not in some dramatic movie way, just losing the thread, getting overwhelmed, not being able to answer things I should know.\n\nMy supervisor actually took me off active project decisions, which was humiliating. I mean, I get why. You can’t have someone unstable making calls about loads and safety and budgets and all that. Sorry, that’s probably too much detail.\n\nAnd at home it’s not better. I stopped keeping up with basic routines, like the normal maintenance stuff of being a person. It got bad enough that I ended up being sent to the emergency department because I didn’t feel safe with my thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I’ve had rough patches before, like after the divorce especially, but I don’t know if it was “depressed like this” or if it lasted exactly two weeks. I’m bad with timelines unless I can anchor them to a project deadline or something concrete.\n\nThere were times I wasn’t doing great, definitely, but this feels… heavier? I can remember being really low at different points, but whether it was nearly every day for two full weeks before this past month… I can’t say clearly.\n\nIt’s weird, because I can remember details like which bridge inspection report was late or what color folder I used for a permit set, but my own mood timeline is just blurry. So I don’t want to say yes if I’m just filling in gaps. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: If the divorce and financial fallout were suddenly resolved, do you think this depressed feeling would still be there?\n\nPatient: I think… yes. I mean, if the money stuff and the divorce mess magically disappeared, I’d obviously be relieved, but I don’t think this feeling would just lift.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in. I can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling. It’s like everything I tried to build back up after the divorce has collapsed anyway, and I’m just sitting underneath it. And it’s hard to explain because part of me wants to say, “Well, of course I’m upset, look at the circumstances,” but it feels bigger than that. Like it would still be there even if someone fixed the practical pieces.\n\nDoctor: Are you having thoughts of killing yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not like I have some neat answer to that. But the feeling is this heavy, hopeless thing, like I’m stuck under everything that fell apart and I can’t really get out from under it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way. I’ve still been doing some things, but it’s hard to tell if I’m enjoying them or just… going through the motions.\n\nLike, I used to spend ages messing with little design projects at home—totally unnecessary stuff, like reorganizing a shelf so it “distributes load better,” which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud. And lately I’ll start something and then get distracted by, I don’t know, a bill sitting on the counter or an email I forgot to answer, and then suddenly I’m cleaning a drawer I didn’t need to clean.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. Maybe? I just don’t feel very connected to much right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think so. It’s this heavy, hopeless feeling I can’t seem to shake.\n\nI came in because it feels like everything I tried to build back up after the divorce just collapsed, and I’m kind of… sitting underneath it. Like when a structure fails and you can see afterward there were too many loads on it, too many weak points, but knowing that doesn’t actually get you out from under the rubble. So yes, I guess—down, depressed, whatever word fits—it’s there a lot of the day.\n\nDoctor: Has this been making it hard for you to function at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. More than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nWork used to be the place where I could sort of hold myself together because there were drawings and numbers and decisions that had a process, you know? But lately I’ve been making calls I probably shouldn’t have been making—questionable ones—and then I’d realize afterward I wasn’t thinking clearly. And meetings… I’ve just kind of fallen apart in them. Not in some dramatic movie way, just losing the thread, getting overwhelmed, not being able to answer things I should know.\n\nMy supervisor actually took me off active project decisions, which was humiliating. I mean, I get why. You can’t have someone unstable making calls about loads and safety and budgets and all that. Sorry, that’s probably too much detail.\n\nAnd at home it’s not better. I stopped keeping up with basic routines, like the normal maintenance stuff of being a person. It got bad enough that I ended up being sent to the emergency department because I didn’t feel safe with my thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I’ve had rough patches before, like after the divorce especially, but I don’t know if it was “depressed like this” or if it lasted exactly two weeks. I’m bad with timelines unless I can anchor them to a project deadline or something concrete.\n\nThere were times I wasn’t doing great, definitely, but this feels… heavier? Or maybe I’m just remembering it that way because I’m in it now. Sorry, I’m kind of circling the question. I don’t really know how to answer that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, was there ever a time in your life when you felt depressed like this nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know. I can remember being really low at different points, but whether it was nearly every day for two full weeks before this past month… I can’t say clearly.\n\nIt’s weird, because I can remember details like which bridge inspection report was late or what color folder I used for a permit set, but my own mood timeline is just blurry. So I don’t want to say yes if I’m just filling in gaps. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: If the divorce and financial fallout were suddenly resolved, do you think this depressed feeling would still be there?\n\nPatient: I think… yes. I mean, if the money stuff and the divorce mess magically disappeared, I’d obviously be relieved, but I don’t think this feeling would just lift.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in. I can’t shake this heavy, hopeless feeling. It’s like everything I tried to build back up after the divorce has collapsed anyway, and I’m just sitting underneath it. And it’s hard to explain because part of me wants to say, “Well, of course I’m upset, look at the circumstances,” but it feels bigger than that. Like it would still be there even if someone fixed the practical pieces.\n\nDoctor: Are you having thoughts of killing yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not like I have some neat answer to that. I’ve been having these thoughts that it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I could just not have to keep doing all of this.\n\nAnd it scared me, honestly. It got intense enough that I called the employee assistance program through work, which was embarrassing because I kept thinking about how I’ve used EAP for workplace trainings before, like those little posters in the break room that everyone ignores… but then I was the person calling.
